Dynamical mean-field analysis of adaptive Langevin diffusions: Propagation-of-chaos and convergence of the linear response
Zhou Fan, Justin Ko, Bruno Loureiro, Yue M. Lu, Yandi Shen

Abstract
Motivated by an application to empirical Bayes learning in high-dimensional regression, we study a class of Langevin diffusions in a system with random disorder, where the drift coefficient is driven by a parameter that continuously adapts to the empirical distribution of the realized process up to the current time. The resulting dynamics take the form of a stochastic interacting particle system having both a McKean-Vlasov type interaction and a pairwise interaction defined by the random disorder. We prove a propagation-of-chaos result, showing that in the large system limit over dimension-independent time horizons, the empirical distribution of sample paths of the Langevin process converges to a deterministic limit law that is described by dynamical mean-field theory. This law is characterized by a system of dynamical fixed-point equations for the limit of the drift parameter and for the correlation and response kernels of the limiting dynamics. Using a dynamical cavity argument, we verify that these correlation and response kernels arise as the asymptotic limits of the averaged correlation and linear response functions of single coordinates of the system. These results enable an asymptotic analysis of an empirical Bayes Langevin dynamics procedure for learning an unknown prior parameter in a linear regression model, which we develop in a companion paper.



1
Introduction

Let
\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

be a system of
d
interacting particles, evolving
according to a stochastic dynamics

\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}^{t}=\Big[{-}\beta\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t}+\big(s(\theta_{j}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t})\big)_{j=1}^{d}\Big]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}^{t},\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\widehat{\alpha}^{t}=\mathcal{G}\Big(\widehat{\alpha}^{t},\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\theta_{j}^{t}}\Big).

(1)

Here
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is a matrix of random disorder,
and
s(\,\cdot\,,\widehat{\alpha}^{t}):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
in the drift coefficient
is a nonlinear function
driven by a stochastic time-dependent parameter
\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
that
adapts to the past history
\{\bm{\theta}^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}
. (We defer formal
definitions and conditions for the functions
s(\cdot)
and
\mathcal{G}(\cdot)
to
Section
2
.) We will study the pathwise convergence of the
empirical measure
\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\theta_{j}^{t}}
and of the
parameter
\widehat{\alpha}^{t}
to deterministic limits as
n,d\to\infty
at a fixed rate, in
this model (
1
) and in a closely related statistical model.

In the setting of
\beta=0
, i.e. with no random disorder,
the dynamics (
1
) take a
pathwise-exchangeable form as studied classically by

[
1
,
2
]
,
where the evolution
\mathrm{d}\theta_{j}^{t}
of each
j^{\text{th}}
particle depends on the remaining particles only via the empirical law

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\{\theta_{j}^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}}
. The
convergence of this law in the asymptotic limit
d\to\infty
, together with a resulting asymptotic
decoupling of low-dimensional marginals of
\{\bm{\theta}^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}
,
is commonly referred to as propagation-of-chaos. We refer to the classical
monographs
[
3
,
4
]
for a detailed treatment
of such models, and to
[
5
,
6
]

and
[
7
,
8
]
for modern surveys and examples
of recent quantitative convergence results.

The study of propagation-of-chaos for dynamics with random disorder (
\beta\neq 0
)
has also a separate and rich
development in the literature, using techniques of dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT).
DMFT was initially developed to study Langevin dynamics in the
soft Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) model

[
9
,
10
]
and related
spherical p-spin models in spin glass theory
[
11
,
12
,
13
]
,
and relied on deep but non-rigorous techniques of the dynamical cavity method

[
14
,
15
]
and generating functional methods

[
16
,
17
,
18
]
of
statistical physics. In recent years, DMFT has been applied to shed insight
into the learning dynamics in an increasingly wide range of
statistical and machine learning models, including matrix and tensor PCA

[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
,
phase retrieval and generalized linear models

[
15
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
, Gaussian
mixture classification
[
27
,
28
]
, and deep neural networks

[
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
]
.

Pioneering work of

[
34
,
35
,
36
,
37
]

established the first mathematical formalizations of DMFT in variants of the SK
model, in the forms of large deviations principles for the empirical
distributions of sample paths. Mathematical results for spherical models were
subsequently obtained in
[
38
,
39
]
, and
universality of such results with respect to the law of the disorder in

[
40
,
41
]
.
Recently,
[
42
]
developed a different and innovative new approach to formalizing DMFT via time discretization and
reduction to Approximate Message Passing schemes
[
43
,
44
,
45
]
, and applied this
to derive a DMFT limit for gradient flow dynamics
in statistical multi-index models. A related strategy via iterative
Gaussian conditioning was developed in
[
46
]
, which
extended the results of
[
42
]
to a class of discrete-time
Langevin and stochastic gradient dynamics. Non-asymptotic analyses of the
entrywise behavior of such dynamics were obtained in
[
47
]
.

In this work, we will prove a DMFT approximation for the dynamics
(
1
), which has both the above elements of a
pathwise-exchangeable interaction driven by the empirical law, as well as a
pairwise interaction driven by random disorder. Our motivation is the study of
a variant of Langevin dynamics for posterior sampling in a statistical linear
model

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{*}+\bm{\varepsilon},\qquad\theta_{j}^{*}\overset{iid}{\sim}g(\,\cdot\,,\alpha^{*}),\qquad\varepsilon_{i}\overset{iid}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}),

where the regression coefficients of interest
\theta_{1}^{*},\ldots,\theta_{d}^{*}

are distributed according to a prior law
g(\,\cdot\,,\alpha^{*})
that has
an unknown parameter
\alpha^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
.
To implement empirical Bayes learning of
\alpha^{*}

[
48
,
49
,
50
]
,
a Langevin dynamics method was introduced in

[
51
]
1
1

1

[
51
]
proposed a
nonparametric variant of this method, and we simplify our discussion here to a
parametric formulation
which,
from an initial estimate or guess
\widehat{\alpha}^{0}
, evolves a prior parameter
estimate

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\widehat{\alpha}^{t}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\nabla_{\alpha}\log g(\theta_{j}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t})

(2)

based on the coordinates of a coupled Langevin diffusion

\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}^{t}=\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\Big({-}\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t}\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\log g(\theta_{j}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t})\Big)\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}^{t}

(3)

that samples from the posterior law

\mathsf{P}(\bm{\theta}\mid\mathbf{X},\mathbf{y})
. Such dynamics comprise a minor extension of
(
1
) (which motivates our choice to study a disorder matrix
having the covariance form
\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}
), and we state in
(
4
–
5
) of Section
2

an extended general dynamics that encompasses this application. We defer a
more detailed discussion and analysis of this specific empirical Bayes procedure
to our companion paper
[
52
]
, focusing in this work
on the formalization of the limiting dynamics in a general context.

We summarize the main contributions of our paper as follows:

1.

Adapting and building upon the methods of
[
42
]
,
we prove a DMFT limit for the dynamics (
1
) (with a natural
extension to the dynamics (
4
–
5
) to follow).
This will take the form of almost-sure convergence to certain deterministic
limits, as
n,d\to\infty
,

\{\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}\to\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\quad\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\{\theta_{j}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}}\overset{W_{2}}{\to}\mathsf{P}(\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}),\quad\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\{\eta_{i}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}}\overset{W_{2}}{\to}\mathsf{P}(\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})

for the sample path of
\widehat{\alpha}^{t}
and for the empirical laws of sample
paths of
\bm{\theta}^{t}
and
\bm{\eta}^{t}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t}
.

Each limit
\mathsf{P}(\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
and
\mathsf{P}(\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
represents the law of a univariate stochastic process, which is
driven by the above limit
\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
for the evolving drift
parameter, an additional Gaussian process representing the mean field,
and an integrated response.
These Gaussian processes and integrated responses are
described by correlation and response kernels
C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
,
where

\{\alpha^{t}\},C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}

are defined self-consistently from the laws

\mathsf{P}(\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
and
\mathsf{P}(\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
via a
system of dynamical fixed-point equations.
We establish that this dynamical fixed point is unique in a certain
domain of functions with exponential growth.

2.

We show that the dynamics (
1
)
admit a well-defined notion of a linear response function
R_{AB}(t,s)

for a class of observables
A,B:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
R_{AB}(t,s)

represents a linear response of
A(\bm{\theta}^{t})
to a perturbation of the
drift coefficient at a previous time
s
by
\nabla B(\bm{\theta}^{s})
.

We then verify that the above DMFT correlation and response kernels

C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
arise as the mean-field limits
of averages of the correlation and linear response
functions for the “single-particle” coordinate observables

A(\bm{\theta}),B(\bm{\theta})=\theta_{j}
and
A(\bm{\theta}),B(\bm{\theta})=[\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}-\mathbf{y}]_{i}

of the high-dimensional system.

Our methods and analyses in the first contribution above follow the approach
of
[
42
]
. We incorporate into the dynamical fixed-point
system a deterministic limit
\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
for the trajectory of
the stochastic drift parameter
\{\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
, extend the
analyses to encompass processes with more irregular sample paths resulting from the
additional Brownian diffusion term
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}^{t}
in the dynamics, and simplify the
approach in
[
42
]
for embedding a discrete-time DMFT system
into a continuous-time limit.

Our second contribution above is, to our knowledge, novel in the mathematical
literature on DMFT (although anticipated by statistical physics derivations
of the DMFT equations). To understand
R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
as asymptotic limits
of averaged single-particle linear response functions, we formalize a
dynamical cavity calculation that analyzes the response of a single
coordinate
\theta_{j}^{t}
to a perturbation of
\theta_{j}^{s}
at a preceding time

s
, via a DMFT approximation of the cavity system with this coordinate left
out. This result will be important to our companion
work
[
52
]
, allowing us to transfer a fluctuation-dissipation theorem

[
53
]
from the
high-dimensional dynamics to the DMFT correlation and response
kernels
C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
. This will then allow us to carry
out an analysis of the long-time behavior of the DMFT equations in an approximately
time-translation-invariant setting, and to show convergence of the prior
parameter estimate
\widehat{\alpha}^{t}
in the above empirical Bayes dynamics to
a stationary point of a replica-symmetric limit for the model free
energy.

Notational conventions

Constants
C,C^{\prime},c,c^{\prime}>0
are independent of the dimensions
n,d
unless otherwise specified, and
may depend on the time horizon
T
, dimension
K
of the drift parameter, and
scalar parameter
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.

In a separable and complete normed vector space
(\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|)
, for
any
p\geq 1
,
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
is the space of probability
distributions
\mathsf{P}
on
(\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|)
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\mathsf{P}}\|\xi\|^{p}<\infty
,
W_{p}(\cdot)
is the Wasserstein-
p
metric on

\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
, and
\mathsf{P}_{n}\overset{W_{p}}{\to}\mathsf{P}
denotes
W_{p}(\mathsf{P}_{n},\mathsf{P})\to 0

as
n\to\infty
. For a
random variable
\xi
in
\mathcal{M}
, we will use
\mathsf{P}(\xi)
to denote its law.
For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{M}^{n}
,

\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathcal{M})
(for any
p\geq 1
) denotes
the empirical distribution of the coordinates
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
of
\mathbf{x}
.

On a Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\|\cdot\|
without subscript is, by convention,
the Euclidean (i.e.
\ell_{2}
) norm.

C([0,T],\mathbb{R})
is the space of continuous
functions
f:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
equipped with the norm of uniform convergence

\|f\|_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|f(t)|
.

\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
denotes the nonnegative integers, and
\mathbb{R}_{+}=[0,\infty)

denotes the nonnegative reals. For a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\nabla f(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are its gradient and
Hessian at
\mathbf{x}
. For
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\mathrm{d}f(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
is its
derivative at
\mathbf{x}
.
\Tr M
,
\|M\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, and
\|M\|_{F}
are the matrix
trace, Euclidean operator norm, and Frobenius norm.

2
Model and main results

2.1
Model and dynamics

Let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be a random matrix with independent entries,
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{*}+\bm{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the observations of a linear model
with regression design
\mathbf{X}
, regression coefficients

\bm{\theta}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and noise
\bm{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Let
s:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Lipschitz drift function,

\mathcal{G}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}^{K}
a Lipschitz
gradient map (where
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is the space of probability measures on

\mathbb{R}
with finite second moment),
\{\mathbf{b}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
a
standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
a scalar parameter.
We will study the dynamics

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}^{t}

\displaystyle=\Big[{-}\beta\,\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t}-\mathbf{y})+\big(s(\theta^{t}_{j},\widehat{\alpha}^{t})\big)_{j=1}^{d}\Big]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}^{t}

(4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{\alpha}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}\Big(\widehat{\alpha}^{t},\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\theta_{j}^{t}}\Big)\mathrm{d}t

(5)

with initial conditions

(\bm{\theta}^{0},\widehat{\alpha}^{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}.

(6)

This encompasses the general dynamics (
1
) and the
application (
2
–
3
)
under a unified model:
Specializing to
\bm{\theta}^{*}=0
and
\bm{\varepsilon}=0
(hence
\mathbf{y}=0
) recovers
(
1
), while specializing to
\beta=\sigma^{-2}
,

s(\theta,\alpha)=\partial_{\theta}\log g(\theta,\alpha)
,
and
\mathcal{G}(\alpha,\mathsf{P})=\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\mathsf{P}}[\nabla_{\alpha}\log g(\theta,\alpha)]

recovers (
2
–
3
).
We will refer to these general dynamics (
4
–
5
)
as an adaptive Langevin diffusion.

We impose the following assumptions on the components of the above
model and dynamics throughout this work.

Assumption 2.1

(Model and initial conditions)
.

(a)

(Asymptotic scaling)

\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\frac{n}{d}=\delta\in(0,\infty)
.

(b)

(Random design)

\mathbf{X}=(x_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
has independent entries satisfying

\mathbb{E}x_{ij}=0
,
\mathbb{E}x_{ij}^{2}=\frac{1}{d}
, and
\|\sqrt{d}x_{ij}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C
for a constant
C>0
where
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}}
is the sub-gaussian norm.

(c)

(Linear model and initial conditions)
\bm{\theta}^{0},\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon}
are independent of
\mathbf{X}
, and

\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{*}+\bm{\varepsilon}
. For some probability distributions

\mathsf{P}(\theta^{*},\theta^{0})
and
\mathsf{P}(\varepsilon)

having finite moment generating functions in a neighborhood of 0,
and for each fixed
p\geq 1
, the entries of
\bm{\theta}^{0},\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon}
satisfy the
Wasserstein-
p
convergence almost surely as
n,d\to\infty
,

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{(\theta^{\ast}_{j},\theta_{j}^{0})}\overset{W_{p}}{\to}\mathsf{P}(\theta^{*},\theta^{0}),\qquad\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\varepsilon_{i}}\overset{W_{p}}{\to}\mathsf{P}(\varepsilon).

(7)

For a deterministic parameter
\alpha^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, almost surely

\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\widehat{\alpha}^{0}=\alpha^{0}
.

Assumption 2.2

(Drift function)
.

s:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}
is twice
continuously-differentiable, and
for some constant
C>0
and all

(\theta,\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}
,

|s(\theta,\alpha)|\leq C(1+|\theta|+\|\alpha\|_{2}),\quad\|\nabla_{(\theta,\alpha)}s(\theta,\alpha)\|_{2},\|\nabla_{(\theta,\alpha)}^{2}s(\theta,\alpha)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C.

(8)

Assumption 2.3

(Gradient map)
.

Let
\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta})=d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\theta_{j}}
denote
the empirical distribution of coordinates of
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and let
\mathcal{G}_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}

be the
k^{\text{th}}
component of
\mathcal{G}
.

(a)

For some constant
C>0
and all
(\alpha,\mathsf{P})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\|\mathcal{G}(\alpha,\mathsf{P})\|_{2}\leq C(1+\|\alpha\|_{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{P}}[\theta^{2}]^{1/2}),\quad\|\mathcal{G}(\alpha,\mathsf{P})-\mathcal{G}(\alpha^{\prime},\mathsf{P}^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq C\big(\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|_{2}+W_{2}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}^{\prime})\big).

(9)

(b)

For each
k=1,\ldots,K
,

(\bm{\theta},\alpha)\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{k}(\alpha,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta}))
is twice
continuously-differentiable, and
for some constant
C>0
and
all
(\bm{\theta},\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}
,

\lVert\nabla_{\alpha}\mathcal{G}_{k}(\alpha,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta}))\rVert_{2}\leq C,\qquad\sqrt{d}\,\lVert\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\mathcal{G}_{k}(\alpha,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta}))\rVert_{2}\leq C,

(10)

\max\Big(d\,\lVert\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{k}(\alpha,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta}))\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},\,\sqrt{d}\,\lVert\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\nabla_{\alpha}\mathcal{G}_{k}(\alpha,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta}))\rVert_{\mathrm{op}},\,\lVert\nabla^{2}_{\alpha}\mathcal{G}_{k}(\alpha,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta}))\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\Big)\leq C.

(11)

Viewing (
4
–
5
) as a joint
diffusion of
(\bm{\theta}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t})
on
\mathbb{R}^{d+K}
, we remark that
(
8
) and (
10
) imply that the
drift function of this joint diffusion is Lipschitz with respect to the
Euclidean norm. Then there exists a unique
solution
\{\bm{\theta}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

to (
4
–
5
) with initial condition
(
6
) that is
adapted to the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\mathcal{F}(\{\mathbf{b}^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]},\bm{\theta}^{0},\widehat{\alpha}^{0},\mathbf{X},\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon})
,
which will be the process of interest in our main results.

2.2
Existence and uniqueness of the DMFT fixed point

In this section we define the DMFT limit for the preceding dynamics
(
4
–
5
). Let

\delta=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\frac{n}{d}
and
\alpha^{0}=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\widehat{\alpha}^{0}
be as in Assumption
2.1
. Let

(\theta^{*},\theta^{0})\sim\mathsf{P}(\theta^{*},\theta^{0})

denote scalar variables with the distribution
(
7
). Let

\{b^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\qquad\{u^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

be univariate mean-zero Gaussian processes independent of each other
and of
(\theta^{*},\theta^{0})
, where
\{b^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a standard Brownian
motion and
\{u^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
has a correlation kernel
C_{\eta}(\cdot)
on

[0,\infty)
, defined
self-consistently below. Let

\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

be a deterministic
continuous process on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
, also defined self-consistently below.
We consider univariate processes
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and

\{\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\}_{t\geq s\geq 0}
adapted to the
filtration

\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\theta}:=\mathcal{F}(\{b^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]},\{u^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]},\theta^{*},\theta^{0})

(in the sense that
\theta^{t}
and
\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}
for all
s\in[0,t]
are
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\theta}
-measurable),
defined by the stochastic differential equations

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\theta^{t}

\displaystyle=\Big[{-}\delta\beta(\theta^{t}-\theta^{*})+s(\theta^{t},\alpha^{t})+\int_{0}^{t}R_{\eta}(t,s)(\theta^{s}-\theta^{\ast})\mathrm{d}s+u^{t}\Big]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}b^{t}\text{ with }\theta^{t}\big|_{t=0}=\theta^{0},

(12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\bigg[{-}\bigg(\delta\beta-\partial_{\theta}s(\theta^{t},\alpha^{t})\bigg)\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}+\int_{s}^{t}R_{\eta}(t,s^{\prime})\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}}}{\partial u^{s}}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\bigg]\mathrm{d}t\text{ with }\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\bigg|_{t=s}=1.

(13)

We clarify that
\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}

is a notation for a univariate process
on
t\in[s,\infty)
, defined via (
13
)
for each
s\geq 0
.

Consider also

\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{P}(\varepsilon),\qquad(w^{*},\{w^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}),

where
\varepsilon
is a scalar variable with the distribution
(
7
), and

(w^{*},\{w^{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
is a univariate mean-zero Gaussian process indexed by

\{*\}\cup[0,\infty)
, independent of
\varepsilon
and
with a correlation kernel
C_{\theta}(\cdot)
on
\{*\}\cup[0,\infty)
also
defined self-consistently below. We consider univariate processes

\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and

\{\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}\}_{t\geq s\geq 0}

adapted to the filtration

\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\eta}:=\mathcal{F}(\{w^{s}\}_{s\leq t},w^{*},\varepsilon),

defined by the integral equations

\displaystyle\eta^{t}

\displaystyle={-}\beta\int_{0}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,s)\big(\eta^{s}+w^{*}-\varepsilon\big)\mathrm{d}s-w^{t},

(14)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}

\displaystyle=\beta\Big[{-}\int_{s}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,s^{\prime})\frac{\partial\eta^{s^{\prime}}}{\partial w^{s}}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}+R_{\theta}(t,s)\Big].

(15)

Again
\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}
is a notation for a univariate
process on
t\in[s,\infty)
, defined by (
15
) for each

s\geq 0
.

The centered Gaussian processes
\{u^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and

(w^{*},\{w^{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
above have correlation kernels

\mathbb{E}[u^{t}u^{s}]=C_{\eta}(t,s),\qquad\mathbb{E}[w^{t}w^{s}]=C_{\theta}(t,s),\qquad\mathbb{E}[w^{t}w^{*}]=C_{\theta}(t,*),\qquad\mathbb{E}[(w^{*})^{2}]=C_{\theta}(*,*).

(16)

Denoting by
\mathsf{P}(\theta^{t})
the law of
\theta^{t}
solving
(
12
),
the above deterministic process
\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and correlation/response kernels

C_{\eta},C_{\theta},R_{\eta},R_{\theta}
are defined for all
t\geq s\geq 0

self-consistently by

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\alpha^{t}=\mathcal{G}(\alpha^{t},\mathsf{P}(\theta^{t}))\text{ with }\alpha^{t}\big|_{t=0}=\alpha^{0},

(17)

\begin{gathered}C_{\theta}(t,s)=\mathbb{E}[\theta^{t}\theta^{s}],\quad C_{\theta}(t,*)=\mathbb{E}[\theta^{t}\theta^{*}],\quad C_{\theta}(*,*)=\mathbb{E}[(\theta^{*})^{2}],\\
C_{\eta}(t,s)=\delta\beta^{2}\,\mathbb{E}[(\eta^{t}+w^{*}-\varepsilon)(\eta^{s}+w^{*}-\varepsilon)],\\
R_{\theta}(t,s)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big],\quad R_{\eta}(t,s)=\delta\beta\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}\Big].\end{gathered}

(18)

We note that the above process

\{\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}\}_{t\geq s\geq 0}
defined by
(
15
)
is in fact deterministic, but we keep the expectation defining

R_{\eta}(t,s)
for symmetry of notation.

The equations (
17
–
18
) should
be understood as fixed-point equations for

\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
,
where the laws of the processes
\{\theta^{t},\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}},u^{t}\}_{t\geq s\geq 0}

and
\{\eta^{t},\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}},w^{t}\}_{t\geq s\geq 0}

defining (
17
–
18
)
are in turn defined by
\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}

via (
12
–
16
).
For each fixed time horizon
T>0
, let
\mathcal{S}(T)
be a space of functions

(\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta})\equiv\{\alpha^{t},C_{\theta}(t,s),C_{\theta}(t,*),C_{\theta}(*,*),C_{\eta}(t,s),R_{\theta}(t,s),R_{\eta}(t,s)\}_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T}

having at most exponential growth, and
\mathcal{S}(T)^{\text{cont}}
a subset of
continuous such functions, whose precise
definitions we defer to Section
3.1
to follow.
The following result establishes existence and uniqueness of a fixed point to
(
17
–
18
) in this space

\mathcal{S}(T)^{\text{cont}}
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, and

2.3
, for any fixed
T>0
:

(a)

For any

(\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta})\in\mathcal{S}(T)

and any realization of the mean-zero Gaussian processes

\{u^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
(w^{*},\{w^{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
satisfying
(
16
) (independent of
(\theta^{*},\theta^{0},\{b^{t}\}_{t\geq 0})

and
\varepsilon
respectively), there exist unique
solutions to (
12
–
13
)
and (
14
–
15
) adapted to

\{\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\theta}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
and
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\eta}\}_{t\in[0,T]}

for times

0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.

(b)

There exists a unique fixed point

(\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta})\in\mathcal{S}(T)
satisfying
(
17
–
18
) for the solution of
part (a). This fixed point belongs to
\mathcal{S}(T)^{\text{cont}}
, and in
particular
\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a deterministic
continuous process on
\mathbb{R}^{K}
.

The proof of Theorem
2.4
is given in Section

3
.
We will call the components of Theorem
2.4
(a–b) the unique
solution of the DMFT system
(
12
–
18
).

2.3
The dynamical mean-field approximation

The following is the first main result of our work,
showing that the preceding solution to the DMFT system
describes the limit of
\{\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}

and empirical distributions of coordinates of
\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}

and
\{\bm{\eta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}\equiv\{\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
solving
(
4
–
5
), for fixed time horizons
T>0

in the limit
n,d\to\infty
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, and

2.3
hold. Denote

\bm{\eta}^{t}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t},\qquad\bm{\eta}^{*}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{*}

let
\theta^{*}
,
\varepsilon
,
\eta^{*}={-}w^{*}
, and
\{\theta^{t},\eta^{t},\alpha^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
be the components of the unique solution to the DMFT system
(
12
–
18
) given by
Theorem
2.4
, and let
\mathsf{P}(\cdot)
denote the law of these components. Then for each fixed
T>0
,
almost surely as
n,d\to\infty
,

(a)

(\widehat{\alpha}^{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\to(\alpha^{t})_{t\in[0,T]}

in
C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{K})
.

(b)

In the sense of Wasserstein-2 convergence over
\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T],\mathbb{R})
and

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T],\mathbb{R})
,

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\theta_{j}^{*},\{\theta_{j}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}}\overset{W_{2}}{\to}\mathsf{P}(\theta^{*},\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}),\qquad\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\eta_{i}^{*},\varepsilon_{i},\{\eta_{i}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}}\overset{W_{2}}{\to}\mathsf{P}(\eta^{*},\varepsilon,\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}).

The proof of Theorem
2.5
is given in Section

4
.
For ease of interpretation, we record here two corollaries of this
result. The first clarifies an implication of the above Wasserstein-2
convergence in terms of the convergence of pseudo-Lipschitz test functions of
finite-dimensional marginals of the processes.

Corollary 2.6
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.5
,
for any fixed
m\geq 1
and times

t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}\in[0,T]
, and for any pseudo-Lipschitz test functions

f_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{m+1}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f_{\eta}:\mathbb{R}^{m+2}\to\mathbb{R}

(i.e. satisfying
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq C\|x-y\|_{2}(1+\|x\|_{2}+\|y\|_{2})
),
almost surely as
n,d\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}f_{\theta}(\theta_{j}^{*},\theta_{j}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta_{j}^{t_{m}})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{E}f_{\theta}(\theta^{*},\theta^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta^{t_{m}})

(19)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{\eta}(\eta_{i}^{*},\varepsilon_{i},\eta_{i}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\eta_{i}^{t_{m}})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{E}f_{\eta}(\eta^{*},\varepsilon,\eta^{t_{1}},\ldots,\eta^{t_{m}})

where the expectations on the right side are under the joint laws of
the solution to the DMFT system.

Proof.

Any pseudo-Lipschitz function
(\theta^{*},\theta^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta^{t_{m}})\mapsto f_{\theta}(\theta^{*},\theta^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta^{t_{m}})
is also a
pseudo-Lipschitz function of the full sample path

(\theta^{*},\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})\in\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T],\mathbb{R})
. Thus the
first statement of (
19
) follows from Theorem

2.5
(b) and the characterization of Wasserstein-
p
convergence
in
[
54
, Definition 6.8 and Theorem 6.9]
, and the second
statement follows similarly.
∎

The second corollary asserts an asymptotic decoupling of the finite-dimensional
marginal distributions of
(\bm{\theta}^{*},\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
in a
coordinate-exchangeable setting, which is the usual notion of
propagation-of-chaos for interacting particle systems.

Corollary 2.7
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.5
, suppose in addition that

(\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 2}
and
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are both invariant in law under permutations
of the coordinates
\{1,\ldots,d\}
.

Fix any
J\geq 1
, and let

\mathsf{P}(\theta_{1:J}^{*},\{\theta_{1:J}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
denote the joint law of
sample paths

(\theta_{j}^{*},\{\theta^{t}_{j}\}_{t\in[0,T]})\in\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T],\mathbb{R})
for

j=1,\ldots,J
. Let
\mathsf{P}(\theta^{*},\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})^{\otimes J}

denote the
J
-fold product of the limit law in Theorem

2.5
(b). Then as
n,d\to\infty
, in the sense of weak
convergence,

\mathsf{P}(\theta_{1:J}^{*},\{\theta_{1:J}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})\to\mathsf{P}(\theta^{*},\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})^{\otimes J}.

Proof.

Under the stated assumptions and the definition of the process
(
4
–
5
),
the law of
(\bm{\theta}^{*},\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})\in(\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T],\mathbb{R}))^{d}
remains invariant under permutations of
the coordinates
\{1,\ldots,d\}
. Then the stated result is equivalent to
convergence of the empirical law
\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\theta_{j}^{*},\{\theta_{j}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}}
to

\mathsf{P}(\theta^{*},\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
weakly in probability
(c.f.
[
4
, Proposition 2.2]
), and this is implied by
Theorem
2.5
(b).
∎

We clarify that
\mathsf{P}(\theta_{1:J}^{*},\{\theta_{1:J}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]})
in this
statement refers to the law over all randomness including that of

\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\theta}^{0}
and the disorder
\mathbf{X}
. It would be interesting to also study propagation-of-chaos phenomena conditional on parts of this
randomness, and we leave such investigations to future work.

2.4
Interpretation of the DMFT correlation and response

Fixing
\mathbf{X},\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{*}+\bm{\varepsilon}
,
define the coordinate observables

e_{j}(\bm{\theta})=\theta_{j},\qquad x_{i}(\bm{\theta})=\sqrt{\delta}\beta([\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}]_{i}-y_{i}).

(20)

Fixing also the initial conditions
\mathbf{x}=(\bm{\theta}^{0},\widehat{\alpha}^{0})
,
for each pair
A,B\in\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, define

\{R_{AB}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,s)\}_{0\leq s\leq t}

as a response function for the joint dynamics
(
4
–
5
) that satisfies the following condition:
For any continuous bounded function

h:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, consider the perturbed dynamics

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}^{t,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\Big[{-}\beta\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t,\varepsilon}-\mathbf{y})+\varepsilon h(t)\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}B(\bm{\theta}^{t,\varepsilon},\widehat{\alpha}^{t,\varepsilon})+\big(s(\theta_{j}^{t,\varepsilon},\widehat{\alpha}^{t,\varepsilon})\big)_{j=1}^{d}\Big]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}^{t}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widehat{\alpha}^{t,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}\Big(\widehat{\alpha}^{t,\varepsilon},\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{\theta_{j}^{t,\varepsilon}}\Big)\mathrm{d}t

with the same initial condition
(\bm{\theta}^{0,\varepsilon},\widehat{\alpha}^{0,\varepsilon})=\mathbf{x}
. Denote the expectation conditional on
\mathbf{X},\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon}

and
\mathbf{x}=(\bm{\theta}^{0},\widehat{\alpha}^{0})
as
\langle f(\{\bm{\theta}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0})\rangle_{\mathbf{x}}
. Then for any
t>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Big(\langle A(\bm{\theta}^{t,\varepsilon},\widehat{\alpha}^{t,\varepsilon})\rangle_{\mathbf{x}}-\langle A(\bm{\theta}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t})\rangle_{\mathbf{x}}\Big)=\int_{0}^{t}R_{AB}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,s)h(s)\,ds.

(21)

Thus
R_{AB}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,s)
may be understood as the linear response of the observable

A(\bm{\theta})
at time
t
to a perturbation of the Langevin
potential by
B(\bm{\theta})
at a preceding time
s
. Existence of such a response function for smooth bounded observables in uniformly elliptic and hypoelliptic diffusions has been shown in
[
55
,
56
]
.
We verify in Proposition
A.1
that the arguments of
[
56
]
may be extended to show also the existence of a response function
R_{AB}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,s)
satisfying (
21
) in our adaptive Langevin diffusion,
for a class of unbounded and
Lipschitz observables including all

A,B\in\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{d},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
.

Let
\{\bm{\theta}^{t},\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the
solution to (
4
–
5
) with the given
initial condition
\mathbf{x}=(\bm{\theta}^{0},\widehat{\alpha}^{0})
of
Assumption
2.1
,
and define the corresponding correlation and response matrices

\begin{gathered}\mathbf{C}_{\theta}(t,s)=\Big(\big\langle e_{j}(\bm{\theta}^{t})e_{k}(\bm{\theta}^{s})\big\rangle_{\mathbf{x}}\Big)_{j,k=1}^{d},\quad\mathbf{C}_{\theta}(t,*)=\Big(\big\langle e_{j}(\bm{\theta}^{t})e_{k}(\bm{\theta}^{*})\big\rangle_{\mathbf{x}}\Big)_{j,k=1}^{d},\quad\mathbf{R}_{\theta}(t,s)=\Big(R_{e_{j}e_{k}}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,s)\Big)_{j,k=1}^{d},\\
\mathbf{C}_{\eta}(t,s)=\Big(\big\langle x_{j}(\bm{\theta}^{t})x_{k}(\bm{\theta}^{s})\big\rangle_{\mathbf{x}}\Big)_{j,k=1}^{n},\quad\mathbf{R}_{\eta}(t,s)=\Big(R_{x_{j}x_{k}}^{\mathbf{x}}(t,s)\Big)_{j,k=1}^{n}\end{gathered}

(22)

for the above coordinate observables

e_{1},\ldots,e_{d},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
The following is the second main result of our work, showing that
the correlation and response kernels
C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
defining
the DMFT limit in Theorem
2.5
are the almost-sure limits of
the normalized traces of these matrices, i.e. the correlation and
self-responses of the observables
e_{j}
and
x_{i}
averaged
across coordinates
j=1,\ldots,d
and
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Suppose Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, and

2.3
hold, and
(\theta,\alpha)\mapsto\nabla_{(\theta,\alpha)}^{2}s(\theta,\alpha)
and
(\bm{\theta},\alpha)\mapsto\nabla_{(\bm{\theta},\alpha)}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{k}(\alpha,\widehat{\mathsf{P}}(\bm{\theta}))
are uniformly Hölder-continuous for each
k=1,\ldots,K
.
Let
C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
be the correlation and response
kernels of the solution to the DMFT system
(
12
–
18
) given by
Theorem
2.4
.
Then for any fixed
t\geq s\geq 0
, almost surely as
n,d\to\infty
,

\begin{gathered}d^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{C}_{\theta}(t,s)\to C_{\theta}(t,s),\qquad d^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{C}_{\theta}(t,*)\to C_{\theta}(t,*),\qquad n^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{C}_{\eta}(t,s)\to C_{\eta}(t,s),\\
d^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\theta}(t,s)\to R_{\theta}(t,s),\qquad n^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\eta}(t,s)\to R_{\eta}(t,s).\end{gathered}

The proof of Theorem
2.8
is provided in Section

5
. We note that
the convergence of
d^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{C}_{\theta}
and
n^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{C}_{\eta}
is an
immediate consequence of Corollary
2.6
.
The additional content of this theorem is
the convergence of
d^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\theta}
and

n^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\eta}
, which relies on an inductive analysis of dynamics at a
single particle level using a dynamical cavity argument.

Remark 2.9
.

By an argument similar to our proof of Theorem
2.8
, one
may show that the DMFT response kernels
R_{\theta}(t,s)
and
R_{\eta}(t,s)
also
represent the limits of
d^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\theta}(t,s)
and
n^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\eta}(t,s)
defined for a non-adaptive version of the dynamics

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bm{\theta}}^{t}=\Big[{-}\beta\mathbf{X}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}\tilde{\bm{\theta}}^{t}-\mathbf{y})+\big(s(\tilde{\theta}^{t}_{j},\alpha^{t})\big)_{j=1}^{d}\Big]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{b}^{t}

which replaces the adaptively-evolving
drift parameter
\{\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
by its
deterministic DMFT limit
\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.
The response matrices
\mathbf{R}_{\theta},\mathbf{R}_{\eta}
for this non-adaptive dynamics

\{\tilde{\bm{\theta}}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

are different from those for the adaptive dynamics
(
4
–
5
), in that a perturbation
in the adaptive system affects
\{\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\geq s}

whereas it does not change
\{\alpha^{t}\}_{t\geq s}
in the non-adaptive
system. However, our result implies that the almost-sure limits of

d^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\theta}
and
n^{-1}\Tr\mathbf{R}_{\eta}
coincide for these two
dynamics, i.e. the propagation of the effect of the perturbation through

\{\widehat{\alpha}^{t}\}
is negligible in the large-
(n,d)
limit.

The remainder of this paper will prove the preceding results of Theorems

2.4
,
2.5
, and
2.8
.

3
Existence and uniqueness of the DMFT fixed point

In this section we prove Theorem
2.4
. We assume throughout Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, and
2.3
.
Section
3.1
defines
the spaces
\mathcal{S}(T)
and
\mathcal{S}(T)^{\text{cont}}
and proves Theorem
2.4
(a) on existence
and uniqueness of the processes
(
12
–
15
).
Section
3.2
then proves
Theorem
2.4
(b) on existence and uniqueness of the dynamical
fixed point via a contractive mapping argument similar to that of
[
42
]
.

3.1
The function spaces
\mathcal{S}(T)
and
\mathcal{S}(T)^{\text{cont}}

Let
\tau_{*}^{2}=\mathbb{E}{\theta^{*}}^{2}
and
\sigma^{2}=\mathbb{E}\varepsilon^{2}
, and let
C_{0}>0

denote a constant larger than the constants
C>0
of (
8
)
and (
9
).
Consider the following system of equations for functions

\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}},\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}

on
[0,\infty)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{\alpha}(t)

\displaystyle=4.1C_{0}(1+\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t))+3C_{0}\Phi_{\alpha}(t)\text{ with }\Phi_{\alpha}(0)=\|\alpha^{0}\|^{2},

(23)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)

\displaystyle=(6\delta^{2}\beta^{2}+18C_{0}^{2}+1.1)\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)+6\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(t-s)\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+6\Big(\delta^{2}\beta^{2}\tau_{*}^{2}+3C_{0}^{2}+3C_{0}^{2}\Phi_{\alpha}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(t-s)\mathrm{d}s\cdot\tau_{*}^{2}+\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)\Big)+2

\displaystyle\text{ with }\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(0)=\mathbb{E}(\theta^{0})^{2},

(24)

\displaystyle\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)

\displaystyle=2\delta\beta^{2}\Big[\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\cdot\Phi^{2}_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s+2\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)+2\tau_{*}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\Big],

(25)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t)

\displaystyle=(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t-s)\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\text{ with }\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(0)=1,

(26)

\displaystyle\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t)

\displaystyle=|\beta|\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\delta|\beta|\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t)\Big).

(27)

Lemma 3.1
.

The system (
23
–
27
) has a unique continuous
solution. Defining

E(\lambda)=\Big\{\text{functions }f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)\text{ such that }\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}f(s)\mathrm{d}s<\infty\Big\},

for any sufficiently large constant
\lambda>0
, this solution satisfies

\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}},\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}\in E(\lambda)
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi_{\eta}=(\Phi_{C_{\eta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}})
,

\Phi_{\theta}=(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\theta}})
, and

\Phi=(\Phi_{\eta},\Phi_{\theta})
. For any two continuous solutions
\Phi
and

\tilde{\Phi}
, there exists some
M>0
such that all components of both solutions are uniformly
bounded over
[0,T]
by
M
. The above equations then imply

\|\Phi_{\theta}(t)-\tilde{\Phi}_{\theta}(t)\|\leq\int_{0}^{t}C\|\Phi(s)-\tilde{\Phi}(s)\|\mathrm{d}s,\quad\|\Phi_{\eta}(t)-\tilde{\Phi}_{\eta}(t)\|\leq C\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\|\Phi(s)-\tilde{\Phi}(s)\|\mathrm{d}s+\|\Phi_{\theta}(t)-\tilde{\Phi}_{\theta}(t)\|\Big)

for a constant
C>0
depending on
M,T
. Applying Gronwall’s lemma to the
second inequality shows

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|\Phi_{\eta}(s)-\tilde{\Phi}_{\eta}(s)\|\leq C^{\prime}\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|\Phi_{\theta}(s)-\tilde{\Phi}_{\theta}(s)\|.

(28)

Then applying this in the first inequality gives

\|\Phi_{\theta}(t)-\tilde{\Phi}_{\theta}(t)\|\leq\int_{0}^{t}C^{\prime\prime}\sup_{r\in[0,s]}\|\Phi_{\theta}(r)-\tilde{\Phi}_{\theta}(r)\|\mathrm{d}s,

so Gronwall’s lemma applied again shows
\Phi_{\theta}(t)=\tilde{\Phi}_{\theta}(t)

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Then by (
28
), also
\Phi(t)=\tilde{\Phi}(t)

for all
t\in[0,T]
, so any continuous solution to
(
23
–
27
) is unique.

It remains to show existence of a continuous solution with all components in

E(\lambda)
.
Consider (
26
–
27
) as a mapping from

\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}
on the right side to

\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}
on the left side, i.e.

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}}(t)

\displaystyle=1+\int_{0}^{t}\Big((\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t^{\prime})+\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t^{\prime}-s)\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\Big)\mathrm{d}{t^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}(t)

\displaystyle=|\beta|\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\delta|\beta|\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t)\Big).

If
\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}
are non-negative, then clearly so are

\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}
. Furthermore,
if
\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}\in E(\lambda)
, then writing

L_{\theta}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(s)e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s
for
the Laplace transform of
\Phi_{R_{\theta}}
and similarly writing
L_{\eta},\tilde{L}_{\theta},\tilde{L}_{\eta}
for those of
\Phi_{R_{\eta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}
, taking Laplace transforms of
the above gives

\displaystyle\lambda\tilde{L}_{\theta}(\lambda)-1

\displaystyle=(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})L_{\theta}(\lambda)+L_{\eta}(\lambda)L_{\theta}(\lambda),

(29)

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{\eta}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=|\beta|L_{\theta}(\lambda)L_{\eta}(\lambda)+\delta\beta^{2}L_{\theta}(\lambda).

This implies in particular that

\tilde{L}_{\theta}(\lambda),\tilde{L}_{\eta}(\lambda)<\infty
, i.e.
\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}\in E(\lambda)
.
For
\iota>0
, define further

E(\lambda,\iota)=\{(\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda)\times E(\lambda):L_{\theta}(\lambda)\leq\iota,\,L_{\eta}(\lambda)\leq(\delta\beta^{2}+1)\iota\}.

If
(\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda,\iota)
, then for
\iota>0
sufficiently
small and
\lambda>0
sufficiently large, this
implies
\tilde{L}_{\theta}(\lambda)\leq\lambda^{-1}(1+(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\iota+(\delta\beta^{2}+1)\iota^{2})\leq\iota
and

\tilde{L}_{\eta}(\lambda)\leq|\beta|(\delta\beta^{2}+1)\iota^{2}+\delta\beta^{2}\iota\leq(\delta\beta^{2}+1)\iota
, so

(\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda,\iota)
.
For two pairs of inputs
(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{1}),(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2})\in E(\lambda,\iota)
,
note also from the above that

\displaystyle|(\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}}^{1}-\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}}^{2})(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\Big[(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})|(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2})(t^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt+\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}\Big(\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{1}(t^{\prime}-s)|(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2})(s)|+|(\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2})(t^{\prime}-s)|\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2}(s)\Big)\mathrm{d}s\Big]\mathrm{d}t^{\prime},

\displaystyle|(\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}^{1}-\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}^{2})(t)|

\displaystyle\leq|\beta|\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1}(t-s)|(\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2})(s)|+|(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2})(t-s)|\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(s)\Big)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt+\delta\beta^{2}|(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2})(t)|.

Denoting by
L_{\theta}^{1},L_{\theta}^{2},L_{\theta}^{\Delta}
the Laplace
transforms of

\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1},\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2},|\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2}|
, and
defining similarly
L_{\eta}^{1},L_{\eta}^{2},L_{\eta}^{\Delta}
for
\Phi_{R_{\eta}}

and
\tilde{L}_{\theta}^{1},\tilde{L}_{\theta}^{2},\tilde{L}_{\theta}^{\Delta},\tilde{L}_{\eta}^{1},\tilde{L}_{\eta}^{2},\tilde{L}_{\eta}^{\Delta}
for the outputs

\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}
, taking Laplace transforms gives

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{\theta}^{\Delta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta|\beta|+C_{0}}{\lambda}L_{\theta}^{\Delta}+\frac{L_{\eta}^{1}}{\lambda}L_{\theta}^{\Delta}+\frac{L_{\theta}^{2}(\delta\beta^{2}+1)}{\lambda}\frac{L_{\eta}^{\Delta}}{\delta\beta^{2}+1}

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{L}_{\eta}^{\Delta}}{\delta\beta^{2}+1}

\displaystyle\leq|\beta|L_{\theta}^{1}\frac{L_{\eta}^{\Delta}}{\delta\beta^{2}+1}+\frac{|\beta|L_{\eta}^{2}}{\delta\beta^{2}+1}L_{\theta}^{\Delta}+\frac{\delta\beta^{2}}{\delta\beta^{2}+1}L_{\theta}^{\Delta}.

Thus, defining a metric on
E(\lambda,\iota)
(with the usual identification of
functions that are equal Lebesgue-a.e.) given by

\displaystyle d((\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{1}),(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}))

\displaystyle=L_{\theta}^{\Delta}(\lambda)+(\delta\beta^{2}+1)^{-1}L_{\eta}^{\Delta}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}|(\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2})(s)|e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s+(\delta\beta^{2}+1)^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}|(\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{1}-\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2})(s)|e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s,

and applying the bounds
L_{\theta}(\lambda)\leq\iota
and
L_{\eta}(\lambda)\leq(\delta\beta^{2}+1)\iota
on
E(\lambda,\iota)
, this shows that the mapping

(\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}})\mapsto(\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}})
is Lipschitz on
E(\lambda,\iota)

with respect to
d(\cdot)
, with Lipschitz constant at most

\frac{\delta|\beta|+C_{0}}{\lambda}+\frac{2(\delta\beta^{2}+1)\iota}{\lambda}+2|\beta|\iota+\frac{\delta\beta^{2}}{\delta\beta^{2}+1}.

This is less than 1
for
\iota>0
sufficiently small and
\lambda>0
sufficiently large, so

(\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}})\mapsto(\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}})
is a contraction on
(E(\lambda,\iota),d(\cdot))
. Since

(E(\lambda,\iota),d(\cdot))
is a closed subset of a weighted
L^{1}
-space, it
is complete, so by the Banach fixed-point theorem, there
exists a fixed point
(\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda,\iota)\subset E(\lambda)\times E(\lambda)
of this mapping.
The functions
\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}}
of this fixed point are
continuous since
\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{R_{\eta}}
of this mapping
are continuous. This establishes that there is a unique continuous solution to
(
26
–
27
) in
E(\lambda)\times E(\lambda)
.

Given this solution to (
26
–
27
), consider now
(
23
–
25
) as a mapping from

(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}})
on the right side to

(\tilde{\Phi}_{\alpha},\tilde{\Phi}_{C_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{C_{\eta}})
on the left side.
Now let
L_{\alpha}(\lambda),L_{\theta}(\lambda),L_{\eta}(\lambda)
denote the
Laplace transforms of
(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}})
, and define
also the Laplace transforms

K_{\eta}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{t}(s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(s)e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s

and
K_{\theta}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{t}(s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{2}(s)e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s
.
Choosing
\lambda
large enough so that

K_{\eta}(\lambda),K_{\theta}(\lambda)<\infty
,
if
\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}}\in E(\lambda)
, then
taking Laplace transforms of (
23
–
25
) gives

\displaystyle\lambda\tilde{L}_{\alpha}(\lambda)-\|\alpha^{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4.1C_{0}}{\lambda}+4.1C_{0}L_{\theta}(\lambda)+3C_{0}L_{\alpha}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\lambda\tilde{L}_{\theta}(\lambda)-\mathbb{E}(\theta^{0})^{2}

\displaystyle=C_{1}L_{\theta}(\lambda)+6K_{\eta}(\lambda)L_{\theta}(\lambda)+18C_{0}^{2}L_{\alpha}(\lambda)+\frac{6\tau_{*}^{2}}{\lambda}K_{\eta}(\lambda)+6L_{\eta}(\lambda)+\frac{C_{2}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{\eta}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=2\beta^{2}K_{\theta}(\lambda)L_{\eta}(\lambda)+4\delta\beta^{2}L_{\theta}(\lambda)+\frac{C_{3}}{\lambda}

for some constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
depending only on

\delta,\beta,C_{0},\sigma^{2},\tau_{*}^{2}
. For small
\iota>0
, suppose further that

(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda,\iota)
where

E(\lambda,\iota)=\{(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda)\times E(\lambda)\times E(\lambda):L_{\alpha}(\lambda)\leq\iota,\,L_{\theta}(\lambda)\leq\iota,\,L_{\eta}(\lambda)\leq(4\delta\beta^{2}+1)\iota\}.

Then, using that
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}K_{\theta}(\lambda)=0

and
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}K_{\eta}(\lambda)=0
, for sufficiently large

\lambda>0
and small
\iota>0
, the above Laplace transform
equations imply
(\tilde{\Phi}_{\alpha},\tilde{\Phi}_{C_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{C_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda,\iota)
. Furthermore, defining the metric

\displaystyle d((\Phi_{\alpha}^{1},\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{1},\Phi_{C_{\eta}}^{1}),(\Phi_{\alpha}^{2},\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{2},\Phi_{C_{\eta}}^{2}))=L_{\alpha}^{\Delta}(\lambda)+L_{\theta}^{\Delta}(\lambda)+(4\delta\beta^{2}+1)L_{\eta}^{\Delta}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}|(\Phi_{\alpha}^{1}-\Phi_{\alpha}^{2})(s)|e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{\infty}|(\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{1}-\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{2})(s)|e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s+(4\delta\beta^{2}+1)\int_{0}^{\infty}|(\Phi_{C_{\eta}}^{1}-\Phi_{C_{\eta}}^{2})(s)|e^{-\lambda s}\mathrm{d}s

it may be verified as above that the mapping

(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}})\mapsto(\tilde{\Phi}_{\alpha},\tilde{\Phi}_{C_{\theta}},\tilde{\Phi}_{C_{\eta}})
is Lipschitz
on
(E(\lambda,\iota),d(\cdot))
, with Lipschitz constant at most

\frac{7.1C_{0}}{\lambda}+\frac{C_{1}+6K_{\eta}(\lambda)+18C_{0}^{2}+6(4\delta\beta^{2}+1)}{\lambda}+2\beta^{2}K_{\theta}(\lambda)+\frac{4\delta\beta^{2}}{4\delta\beta^{2}+1}.

For sufficiently large
\lambda>0
, this is again less than 1, so there exists a
unique fixed point
(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}})\in E(\lambda,\iota)\subset E(\lambda)\times E(\lambda)\times E(\lambda)
and
hence a unique continuous solution to (
23
–
25
).
∎

Let
(\Phi_{\alpha},\Phi_{C_{\theta}},\Phi_{C_{\eta}},\Phi_{R_{\theta}},\Phi_{R_{\eta}})

be the above solution to (
23
–
27
).
For any
T>0
and
finite set
D=\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}\}\subset(0,T)
, we call

[0,d_{1}),[d_{1},d_{2}),\ldots,[d_{m},T]
the maximal intervals of
[0,T]\setminus D
.
Fixing
T>0
and denoting

(\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta})\equiv\{\alpha^{t},C_{\theta}(t,s),C_{\theta}(t,*),C_{\theta}(*,*),C_{\eta}(t,s),R_{\theta}(t,s),R_{\eta}(t,s)\}_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T},

we define the space
\mathcal{S}\equiv\mathcal{S}(T)
in Theorem
2.4
as

\mathcal{S}=\{(\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta}):(R_{\eta},C_{\eta},\alpha)\in\mathcal{S}_{\eta},\,(R_{\theta},C_{\theta},\alpha)\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}\}.

(30)

Here
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}\equiv\mathcal{S}_{\eta}(T)
is the collection of
(R_{\eta},C_{\eta},\alpha)
such that,
for some (possibly empty) discontinuity set
D\subset(0,T)
of at most
finite cardinality:

•

C_{\eta}
is a positive-semidefinite covariance kernel on
[0,T]

(identifying
C_{\eta}(s,t)=C_{\eta}(t,s)
) satisfying

\displaystyle C_{\eta}(t,t)\leq\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq T.

(31)

Furthermore,
C_{\eta}(t,s)
is uniformly continuous over
s,t\in I

for each maximal interval
I
of
[0,T]\setminus D
, and satisfies

\displaystyle\big|C_{\eta}(t,t)-2C_{\eta}(t,s)+C_{\eta}(s,s)\big|

\displaystyle\leq 3\beta^{2}\bigg[\Big(T^{3}\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)^{2}+T\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(r)^{2}\Big)\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(r)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt+\delta\Big(2T\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)+4\Big)\bigg]\cdot|t-s|\text{ for all }s,t\in I.

(32)

•

R_{\eta}(t,s)
satisfies

\displaystyle|R_{\eta}(t,s)|\leq\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t-s)\text{ for all }0\leq s\leq t\leq T.

(33)

Furthermore,
R_{\eta}(t,s)
is uniformly continuous over
s\in I^{\prime}
and
t\in I

for any two (possibly equal) maximal intervals
I,I^{\prime}
of
[0,T]\setminus D
.

•

\alpha^{t}
satisfies

\|\alpha^{t}\|^{2}\leq\Phi_{\alpha}(t)\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq T.

(34)

and is uniformly continuous on each
maximal interval
I
of
[0,T]\setminus D
.

Similarly
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}\equiv\mathcal{S}_{\theta}(T)
is the set of
(R_{\theta},C_{\theta},\alpha)
such that

•

C_{\theta}
is a positive-semidefinite covariance kernel on
\{*\}\cup[0,T]

(identifying
C_{\theta}(s,t)=C_{\theta}(t,s)
and
C_{\theta}(t,*)=C_{\theta}(*,t)
) satisfying

\displaystyle C_{\theta}(t,t)

\displaystyle\leq\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)\text{ for all }0\leq t\leq T.

(35)

Furthermore,
C_{\theta}(t,s)
is uniformly continuous over
s,t\in I

for each maximal interval
I
of
[0,T]\setminus D
and satisfies

\displaystyle\big|C_{\theta}(t,t)-2C_{\theta}(t,s)+C_{\theta}(s,s)\big|\leq\Big(2T\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)+4\Big)|t-s|\text{ for all }s,t\in I,

(36)

and
C_{\theta}(t,*)
is uniformly continuous over
t\in I
.

•

R_{\theta}(t,s)
satisfies

\displaystyle|R_{\theta}(t,s)|\leq\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)\text{ for all }0\leq s\leq t\leq T.

(37)

Furthermore,
R_{\theta}(t,s)
is uniformly continuous over
s\in I^{\prime}
and
t\in I
for any two (possibly equal) maximal intervals
I,I^{\prime}
of
[0,T]\setminus D
,
and satisfies

\displaystyle\big|R_{\theta}(t^{\prime},s)-R_{\theta}(t,s)\big|\leq\Big(\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)\Big)|t^{\prime}-t|\text{ for each fixed }s\in[0,T]\text{ and all }t,t^{\prime}\in[s,T]\cap I.

(38)

•

\alpha^{t}
satisfies (
34
) and is
uniformly continuous on each maximal interval
I
of
[0,T]\setminus D
.

We define

\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}(T)\equiv\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}\subset\mathcal{S},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\eta}^{\text{cont}}(T)\equiv\mathcal{S}_{\eta}^{\text{cont}}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\eta},\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{\text{cont}}(T)\equiv\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{\text{cont}}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\theta}

as the subsets of the above spaces where
D=\emptyset
,
i.e. the above continuity conditions hold on all of
[0,T]
.

Remark 3.2
.

By (
32
), letting
\{u^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
be a
mean-zero Gaussian process with covariance
C_{\eta}
, for any maximal interval

I
of
[0,T]\setminus D
, any
s,t\in I
, and some constant
C>0
,

\mathbb{E}(u^{t}-u^{s})^{4}=3[\mathbb{E}(u^{t}-u^{s})^{2}]^{2}\leq C|t-s|^{2}.

Then Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem (
[
57
, Theorem
2.9]
) implies that there exists a modification of

\{u^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
that is
uniformly Hölder continuous on each such maximal interval
I
, and similarly
for
\{w^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
with covariance
C_{\theta}
satisfying
(
36
). We will always take
\{u^{t}\}
and

\{w^{t}\}
to be the versions of these processes
that satisfy this Hölder continuity.

Let us now establish existence and uniqueness of the solutions to
(
12
–
15
) given

(\alpha,C_{\theta},C_{\eta},R_{\theta},R_{\eta})\in\mathcal{S}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Fix any
T>0
, any
(R_{\eta},C_{\eta},\alpha)\in\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
, and any realizations of
\theta^{0},\theta^{*},\{b^{t}\}_{t\leq T}
and
\{u^{t}\}_{t\leq T}
. Then there exist
unique
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\theta}
-adapted processes
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\leq T}
and

\{\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\}_{s\leq t\leq T}

solving (
12
–
13
).

Proof.

Consider the drift function

v(t,\{\theta^{s}\}_{s\leq t})={-}\delta\beta(\theta^{t}-\theta^{*})+s(\theta^{t},\alpha^{t})+\int_{0}^{t}R_{\eta}(t,s)(\theta^{s}-\theta^{*})\mathrm{d}s+u^{t}.

Conditioning on
\theta^{0},\theta^{*}
and
\{u^{t}\}
and writing 0 for the process

\theta^{t}\equiv 0
, we have (with probability 1 over

\theta^{0},\theta^{*}
and
\{u^{t}\}
)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|v(t,0)|\leq\delta|\beta\theta^{*}|+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|s(0,\alpha^{t})|+\int_{0}^{T}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\cdot|\theta^{*}|+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|u^{t}|<\infty.

Furthermore, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle|v(t,\{\theta^{s}\}_{s\leq t})-v(t,\{\tilde{\theta}^{s}\}_{s\leq t})|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\delta|\beta|+\sup_{(\theta,\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}}|\partial_{\theta}s(\theta,\alpha)|+\int_{0}^{T}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\Big)\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|\theta^{s}-\tilde{\theta}^{s}|,

showing under Assumption
2.2
that

\{\theta^{s}\}_{s\leq t}\mapsto v(t,\{\theta^{s}\}_{s\leq t})
is
Lipschitz in the norm of uniform convergence, uniformly over
t\in[0,T]
.
Then existence and uniqueness of a solution

\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\leq T}
with
\theta^{t}|_{t=0}=\theta^{0}

adapted to the filtration of
\{b^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

is classical, see e.g.
[
58
, Theorem 11.2]
.
This solution is a measurable function of
\theta^{0}
,
\theta^{*}
, and

\{u^{t}\}
, and hence is also
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\theta}
-adapted.

Conditioning now on
\{\theta^{t}\}
, for any fixed
s\in[0,T]
, consider

v(t,\{x^{s^{\prime}}\}_{s^{\prime}\in[s,t]})={-}\Big(\delta\beta-\partial_{\theta}s(\theta^{t},\alpha^{t})\Big)x^{t}+\int_{s}^{t}R_{\eta}(t,s^{\prime})x^{s^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

This satisfies
v(t,0)\equiv 0
for all
t\in[s,T]
and

\displaystyle|v(t,\{x^{s^{\prime}}\}_{s^{\prime}\in[s,t]})-v(t,\{\tilde{x}^{s^{\prime}}\}_{s^{\prime}\in[s,t]})|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\delta|\beta|+\sup_{(\theta,\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{K}}|\partial_{\theta}s(\theta,\alpha)|+\int_{0}^{T}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\Big)\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[s,t]}|x^{s^{\prime}}-\tilde{x}^{s^{\prime}}|,

so
\{x^{s^{\prime}}\}_{s^{\prime}\in[s,t]}\mapsto v(t,\{x^{s^{\prime}}\}_{s^{\prime}\in[s,t]})
is
also Lipschitz in the norm of uniform convergence,
uniformly over
t\in[s,T]
. Then again for each
s\in[0,T]
,
there exists a unique solution

\{\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\}_{t\in[s,T]}

with
\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}|_{t=s}=1
, which is adapted to the
filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}\equiv\mathcal{F}(\{\theta^{r}\}_{r\in[s,t]})
and hence also to

\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\theta}
, showing the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Fix any
T>0
, any

(R_{\theta},C_{\theta},\alpha)\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
, and any realizations of
\varepsilon
and

(w^{*},\{w^{t}\}_{t\leq T})
. Then there exist
unique
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\eta}
-adapted processes
\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\leq T}
and

\{\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}\}_{s\leq t\leq T}

solving (
14
–
15
).

Proof.

Conditional on
\varepsilon
and
(w^{*},\{w^{t}\})
, the equations
(
14
–
15
) are linear
Volterra integral equations for which the kernel
(s,t)\mapsto R_{\theta}(t,s)

is continuous on each maximal interval
I
of
[0,T]\setminus D
. Then, for
each maximal interval
I=[a,b)
, given the values of
\{\eta^{t}\}
for

t\in[0,a]
, existence and uniqueness of
\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\in[a,b)}
is
classical and follows from e.g.
[
59
, Theorem 2.1.2]
.
Applying this successively to each maximal interval
I
shows existence
and uniqueness of
\{\eta^{t}\}
over
t\in[0,T]
. A similar argument shows, for each fixed
s\in[0,T]
, the existence and uniqueness of

\{\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}\}
over
t\in[s,T]
. Here

\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}
is deterministic by its definition,
while
\eta^{t}
is a measurable function of
\varepsilon,w^{*},\{w^{s}\}_{s\leq t}
and
hence is adapted to
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\eta}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.4
(a).

This follows from Lemmas
3.3

and
3.4
.
∎

3.2
Contractive mapping

We fix
T>0
. For any
(R_{\eta},C_{\eta},\alpha)\in\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
, define a map

\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\theta}:(R_{\eta},C_{\eta},\alpha)\rightarrow(R_{\theta},C_{\theta},\tilde{\alpha})
by

\begin{gathered}R_{\theta}(t,s)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big],\quad C_{\theta}(t,s)=\mathbb{E}[\theta^{t}\theta^{s}],\quad C_{\theta}(t,*)=\mathbb{E}[\theta^{t}\theta^{\ast}],\quad C_{\theta}(*,*)=\mathbb{E}[(\theta^{\ast})^{2}],\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\tilde{\alpha}^{t}=\mathcal{G}(\tilde{\alpha}^{t},\mathsf{P}(\theta^{t}))\text{ with }\tilde{\alpha}^{t}|_{t=0}=\alpha^{0}\end{gathered}

where
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
and

\{\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\}_{0\leq s\leq t\leq T}
are the
unique solutions to
(
12
–
13
) given

(R_{\eta},C_{\eta},\alpha)
and
\theta^{0},\theta^{*},\{u^{t}\}
, guaranteed by Lemma
3.3
,
and
\mathsf{P}(\theta^{t})
is the law of
\theta^{t}
.
Similarly, for any
(R_{\theta},C_{\theta},\tilde{\alpha})\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
, define a map

\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\eta}:(R_{\theta},C_{\theta},\tilde{\alpha})\rightarrow(R_{\eta},C_{\eta},\alpha)

by

\displaystyle R_{\eta}(t,s)=\delta\beta\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}\Big],\quad C_{\eta}(t,s)=\delta\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}[(\eta^{t}+w^{\ast}-\varepsilon)(\eta^{s}+w^{\ast}-\varepsilon)],\quad\alpha^{t}=\tilde{\alpha}^{t}

where
\{\eta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
and
\{\frac{\partial\eta^{t}}{\partial w^{s}}\}_{0\leq s<t\leq T}
are the unique solutions to
(
14
–
15
) given

(R_{\theta},C_{\theta},\tilde{\alpha})
and
\varepsilon,w^{*},\{w^{t}\}
,
guaranteed by Lemma
3.4
.
Finally, define the composite maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\eta}=\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\eta}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\theta},\qquad\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\theta}=\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\theta}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\eta}.

(39)

The rest of this subsection is divided into two parts:

•

(
Part 1
) We show in Lemma
3.5
(resp. Lemma
3.6
) that
\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\eta}
maps
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
into
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\theta}
maps
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
into
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
).

•

(
Part 2
) We equip
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
with certain
metrics and derive the moduli-of-continuity of the maps
\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\theta}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\eta}
in Lemmas

3.7
and
3.8
, thereby
concluding that
\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\eta}

and
\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\theta}
in
(
39
) are contractions under these metrics.

Lemma 3.5
.

\mathcal{T}_{\theta\rightarrow\eta}
maps
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
into
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
, and

\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{\text{cont}}
into
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}^{\text{cont}}
.

Proof.

(
Condition for
C_{\eta}
) Define
\xi^{t}=\eta^{t}+w^{\ast}-\varepsilon
, so
that

\displaystyle\xi^{t}=-\beta\int_{0}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,s)\xi^{s}\mathrm{d}s-w^{t}+w^{\ast}-\varepsilon

and
C_{\eta}(t,s)=\delta\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}[\xi^{t}\xi^{s}]
. Then by Cauchy-Schwarz,

\displaystyle C_{\eta}(t,t)

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\beta^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\beta^{2}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,s)\xi^{s}\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{2}+(w^{t}-w^{\ast}+\varepsilon)^{2}\Big]

(40)

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\beta^{2}\,\Big[\beta^{2}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\cdot R_{\theta}(t,s)^{2}\mathbb{E}(\xi^{s})^{2}\mathrm{d}s\cdot\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{-2}\mathrm{d}s+2C_{\theta}(t,t)+2\tau_{*}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\beta^{2}\Big[\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\cdot\Phi^{2}_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)C_{\eta}(s,s)\mathrm{d}s+2\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)+2\tau_{*}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\Big].

Recalling the equation for
\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(\cdot)
in (
25
),

\displaystyle\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)=2\delta\beta^{2}\Big[\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\cdot\Phi^{2}_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s+2\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)+2\tau_{*}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\Big],

Gronwall’s inequality implies that
C_{\eta}(t,t)\leq\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)
,
showing (
31
).

We now check (
32
) on each maximal interval
I
of
[0,T]\setminus D
where
D\subset(0,T)
is the discontinuity set of
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
. Note that

\displaystyle C_{\eta}(t,t)-2C_{\eta}(t,s)+C_{\eta}(s,s)=\delta\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}[(\xi^{t}-\xi^{s})^{2}]

\displaystyle=\delta\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big({-}\beta\int_{0}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,r)\xi^{r}\mathrm{d}r+\beta\int_{0}^{s}R_{\theta}(s,r)\xi^{r}\mathrm{d}r-w^{t}+w^{s}\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 3\delta\beta^{2}\Big[\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{s}\big(R_{\theta}(t,r)-R_{\theta}(s,r)\big)\xi^{r}\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{2}+\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{s}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,r)\xi^{r}\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{2}+\mathbb{E}(w^{t}-w^{s})^{2}\Big].

Using
\delta\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}(\xi^{t})^{2}=C_{\eta}(t,t)\leq\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)
established above, together with
the continuity conditions (
38
) for
R_{\theta}
and
(
36
) for
C_{\theta}
, for any
s,t\in I

it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{s}\big(R_{\theta}(t,r)-R_{\theta}(s,r)\big)\xi^{r}\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq s\int_{0}^{s}(R_{\theta}(t,r)-R_{\theta}(s,r))^{2}\mathbb{E}(\xi^{r})^{2}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T^{2}}{\delta\beta^{2}}\Big(\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)\Big)^{2}\cdot\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(r)\cdot|t-s|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{s}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,r)\xi^{r}\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(t-s)\int_{s}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,r)^{2}\mathbb{E}(\xi^{r})^{2}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta\beta^{2}}\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(r)^{2}\cdot\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(r)\cdot|t-s|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(w^{t}-w^{s})^{2}

\displaystyle=C_{\theta}(t,t)-2C_{\theta}(t,s)+C_{\theta}(s,s)\leq(2T\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)+4)\cdot|t-s|.

Combining these bounds shows (
32
) over
s,t\in I
.
Applying
|\mathbb{E}[\xi^{s}\xi^{t}-\xi^{s^{\prime}}\xi^{t^{\prime}}]|^{2}\leq 2\mathbb{E}(\xi^{s}-\xi^{s^{\prime}})^{2}\mathbb{E}(\xi^{t^{\prime}})^{2}+2\mathbb{E}(\xi^{s^{\prime}})^{2}\mathbb{E}(\xi^{t}-\xi^{t^{\prime}})^{2}
, this shows also that
C_{\eta}(t,s)
is
uniformly continuous over
s,t\in I
. If
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},\tilde{\alpha})\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{\text{cont}}
, then
D=\emptyset
so this maximal interval is
I=[0,T]
.

(
Condition for
R_{\eta}
) By definition,

R_{\eta}(t,s)=\beta[{-}\int_{s}^{t}R_{\theta}(t,s^{\prime})R_{\eta}(s^{\prime},s)\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}+\delta\beta R_{\theta}(t,s)]
, hence

\displaystyle|R_{\eta}(t,s)|

\displaystyle\leq|\beta|\Big(\int_{s}^{t}|R_{\theta}(t,s^{\prime})||R_{\eta}(s^{\prime},s)|\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}+\delta|\beta R_{\theta}(t,s)|\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|\beta|\Big(\int_{0}^{t-s}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s-s^{\prime})|R_{\eta}(s+s^{\prime},s)|\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}+\delta|\beta|\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)\Big).

Recalling the equation for
\Phi_{R_{\eta}}
in (
27
),

\displaystyle\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t-s)=|\beta|\Big(\int_{0}^{t-s}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s-s^{\prime})\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(s^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}+\delta|\beta|\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)\Big),

this implies for all
t\in[s,T]
that

|R_{\eta}(t,s)|\leq\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t-s)
, verifying
(
33
). To show uniform continuity on each pair of maximal
intervals
I,I^{\prime}
defining
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}
,
observe first that for any
s,s^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
\tau\geq 0

for which
s+\tau,s^{\prime}+\tau\in I
,

\displaystyle|R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime})-R_{\eta}(s+\tau,s)|

\displaystyle\leq|\beta|\cdot\left|\int_{s}^{s+\tau}R_{\theta}(s+\tau,r)R_{\eta}(r,s)\mathrm{d}r-\int_{s^{\prime}}^{s^{\prime}+\tau}R_{\theta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,r)R_{\eta}(r,s^{\prime})\mathrm{d}r\right|+\delta\beta^{2}|R_{\theta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime})-R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s)|

\displaystyle=|\beta|\int_{0}^{\tau}\Big|R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s+r)R_{\eta}(s+r,s)-R_{\theta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime}+r)R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+r,s^{\prime})\Big|\mathrm{d}r+\delta\beta^{2}|R_{\theta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime})-R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s)|

\displaystyle\leq|\beta|\int_{0}^{\tau}|R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s+r)|\cdot|R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+r,s^{\prime})-R_{\eta}(s+r,s)|\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt+|\beta|\int_{0}^{\tau}|R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s+r)-R_{\theta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime}+r)|\cdot|R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+r,s^{\prime})|\mathrm{d}r+\delta\beta^{2}|R_{\theta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime})-R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s)|.

Denoting by
o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)
an error that converges to 0 uniformly in
\tau

as
|s-s^{\prime}|\to 0
, observe that the last term above is
o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)

by the uniform continuity of
R_{\theta}
on
I^{\prime}\times I
.
For the second term, writing the range of integration as
[0,\tau]=A\cup B

where
r\in A
are the values for which
s+r,s^{\prime}+r
belong to a single maximal interval of
[0,T]\setminus D
and
r\in B
are the values for which
s+r,s^{\prime}+r
belong to two different maximal intervals, the integral over
r\in A
is
o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)

again by the continuity of
R_{\theta}
, while the integral over
r\in B
is also
o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)
by the boundedness of
R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
and the bound
|B|\leq C|s-s^{\prime}|
for the total length of
B
.
Putting this together,

|R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime})-R_{\eta}(s+\tau,s)|\leq C\int_{0}^{\tau}|R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+r,s^{\prime})-R_{\eta}(s+r,s)|\mathrm{d}r+o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1).

Since
R_{\eta}(s^{\prime},s^{\prime})=R_{\eta}(s,s)
,
the above and Gronwall’s inequality imply that

|R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime})-R_{\eta}(s+\tau,s)|=o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)

(41)

uniformly in
\tau
. Now for any
s\in I^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau\geq 0
for which
s+\tau,s+\tau^{\prime}\in I
,

\displaystyle|R_{\eta}(s+\tau^{\prime},s)-R_{\eta}(s+\tau,s)|

\displaystyle\leq|\beta|\int_{s}^{s+\tau}|R_{\theta}(s+\tau^{\prime},r)-R_{\theta}(s+\tau,r)||R_{\eta}(r,s)|\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\int_{s+\tau}^{s+\tau^{\prime}}|R_{\theta}(s+\tau^{\prime},r)|\cdot|R_{\eta}(r,s)|\mathrm{d}r+\delta|\beta||R_{\theta}(s+\tau^{\prime},s)-R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s)|,

so the continuity of
R_{\theta}

and boundedness of
R_{\theta},R_{\eta}
again imply that

|R_{\eta}(s+\tau^{\prime},s)-R_{\eta}(s+\tau,s)|=o_{|\tau-\tau^{\prime}|}(1)

(42)

uniformly in
s
.
The statements (
41
) and (
42
) show
that
(s,\tau)\mapsto R_{\eta}(s+\tau,s)
is uniformly
continuous over
\{(s,\tau):s\in I^{\prime},\tau\geq 0,s+\tau\in I\}
, implying uniform
continuity of
(s,t)\mapsto R_{\eta}(t,s)
over
(s,t)\in I^{\prime}\times I
.
Again if
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},\tilde{\alpha})\in\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{\text{cont}}
, then this continuity holds over all of
I=[0,T]
.

(
Condition for
\alpha
) By definition, the mapping

\tilde{\alpha}\mapsto\alpha
under
\mathcal{T}_{\theta\to\eta}
is the identity,
so the required conditions for
\alpha
hold by those assumed for
\tilde{\alpha}
.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

\mathcal{T}_{\eta\rightarrow\theta}
maps
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
into
\mathcal{S}_{\theta}^{\text{cont}}
.

Proof.

(
Condition for
C_{\theta}
) To verify (
35
), denote

\displaystyle v^{t}={-}\delta\beta(\theta^{t}-\theta^{*})+s(\theta^{t},\alpha^{t})+\int_{0}^{t}R_{\eta}(t,s)(\theta^{s}-\theta^{*})\mathrm{d}s+u^{t}.

Applying Ito’s formula to
(\theta^{t})^{2}
yields

\frac{\mathrm{d}C_{\theta}(t,t)}{\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{E}(\theta^{t})^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\mathbb{E}[2\theta^{t}v^{t}]+2\leq 1.1\cdot\mathbb{E}(\theta^{t})^{2}+\mathbb{E}(v^{t})^{2}+2.

(43)

(The bound holds with 1 in place of 1.1, and we enlarge this to 1.1 to
accommodate a later discretized version of this computation.)
Using an argument similar to (
40
),
and letting
C_{0}>0
be the constant defining
(
23
–
27
) which upper bounds

C>0
in (
8
) of Assumption
2.2
,
we may bound
\mathbb{E}(v^{t})^{2}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(v^{t})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 6\bigg[\delta^{2}\beta^{2}\mathbb{E}(\theta^{t})^{2}+\delta^{2}\beta^{2}\tau_{*}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[s(\theta^{t},\alpha^{t})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}R_{\eta}(t,s)\theta^{s}\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{2}+\mathbb{E}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}R_{\eta}(t,s)\theta^{*}\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{2}+\mathbb{E}(u^{t})^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq(6\delta^{2}\beta^{2}+18C_{0}^{2})\mathbb{E}(\theta^{t})^{2}+6\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(t-s)\mathbb{E}(\theta^{s})^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+6\Big(\delta^{2}\beta^{2}\tau_{*}^{2}+3C_{0}^{2}+3C_{0}^{2}\Phi_{\alpha}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(t-s)\mathrm{d}s\cdot\tau_{*}^{2}+\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)\Big).

(44)

Applying this to (
43
) and
comparing with the equation for
\Phi_{C_{\theta}}
from (
24
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)

\displaystyle=(6\delta^{2}\beta^{2}+18C_{0}^{2}+1.1)\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)+6\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(t-s)\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s

(45)

\displaystyle\hskip 36.135pt+6\Big(\delta^{2}\beta^{2}\tau_{*}^{2}+3C_{0}^{2}+3C_{0}^{2}\Phi_{\alpha}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s+1)^{2}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}^{2}(t-s)\mathrm{d}s\cdot\tau_{*}^{2}+\Phi_{C_{\eta}}(t)\Big)+2,

we see that since
C_{\theta}(0,0)=\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(0)
, we have

C_{\theta}(t,t)\leq\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)
.

Next we prove (
36
) for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.
We have
\theta^{t}-\theta^{s}=\int_{s}^{t}v^{r}\,\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{2}(b^{t}-b^{s})
.
Then it holds that

\displaystyle C_{\theta}(t,t)-2C_{\theta}(t,s)+C_{\theta}(s,s)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\theta^{t}-\theta^{s})^{2}]\leq 2|t-s|\int_{s}^{t}\mathbb{E}(v^{r})^{2}\mathrm{d}r+4\mathbb{E}(b^{t}-b^{s})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2|t-s|^{2}\sup_{r\in[0,T]}|\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)|+4|t-s|\leq\Big(2T\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r)+4\Big)|t-s|,

where the second inequality compares (
44
) to the definition of
\Phi_{C_{\theta}}^{\prime}(t)
in
(
45
).
This verifies (
36
).
As in the preceding argument for
C_{\eta}
, this condition
(
36
) and Cauchy-Schwarz implies that
C_{\theta}(t,s)

is uniformly continuous over all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, and also that
C_{\theta}(t,*)
is uniformly continuous over all
t\in[0,T]
.

(
Condition for
R_{\theta}
) Let
\bar{R}_{\theta}(t,s)=\mathbb{E}|\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}|
so that
|R_{\theta}(t,s)|\leq\bar{R}_{\theta}(t,s)
by definition. Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big|\leq\Big|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\big(\delta|\beta|+|\partial_{\theta}s(\theta^{t},\alpha^{t})|\big)\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big|+\int_{s}^{t}|R_{\eta}(t,s^{\prime})|\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big|\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big|+\int_{s}^{t}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t-s^{\prime})\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big|\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

(46)

where
C_{0}>0
is the constant defining (
23
–
27
)
which upper bounds
C>0
in (
8
)
of Assumption
2.2
. Taking expectation on both sides yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bar{R}_{\theta}(t,s)\leq(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\bar{R}_{\theta}(t,s)+\int_{0}^{t-s}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t-s-s^{\prime})\bar{R}_{\theta}(s+s^{\prime},s)\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

Recall the equation for
\Phi_{R_{\theta}}
in (
26
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)=(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)+\int_{0}^{t-s}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(t-s-s^{\prime})\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(s^{\prime})\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}.

Since
\bar{R}_{\theta}(s,s)=1=\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(0)
, this implies for all
t\in[s,T]
that

|R_{\theta}(t,s)|\leq\bar{R}_{\theta}(t,s)\leq\Phi_{R_{\theta}}(t-s)
,
verifying (
37
) for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.

To show (
38
) for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq t^{\prime}\leq T
,
observe that we have

\displaystyle|R_{\theta}(t^{\prime},s)-R_{\theta}(t,s)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\Big[{-}\Big(\delta\beta-\partial_{\theta}s(\theta^{r},\alpha^{r})\Big)\frac{\partial\theta^{r}}{\partial u^{s}}\Big]\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}\Big(\int_{s}^{r}R_{\eta}(r,r^{\prime})R_{\theta}(r^{\prime},s)\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\Big)\mathrm{d}r\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{t^{\prime}}\Big((\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\bar{R}_{\theta}(r,s)\mathrm{d}r+\int_{s}^{r}\Phi_{R_{\eta}}(r-r^{\prime})\bar{R}_{\theta}(r^{\prime},s)\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\Big)\mathrm{d}r\leq|t^{\prime}-t|\cdot\sup_{r\in[0,T]}\Phi_{R_{\theta}}^{\prime}(r),

verifying (
38
). In particular, this shows
continuity of
\tau\mapsto R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s)
uniformly over
s\in[0,T]
and
\tau\in[0,T-s]
. For
continuity in
s
, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s+\tau}}{\partial u^{s}}-\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}+\tau}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq(\delta|\beta|+C_{0})\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s+\tau}}{\partial u^{s}}-\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}+\tau}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}\Big|+\int_{0}^{\tau}\Big|R_{\eta}(s+\tau,s+r)\frac{\partial\theta^{s+r}}{\partial u^{s}}-R_{\eta}(s^{\prime}+\tau,s^{\prime}+r)\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}+\tau}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}\Big|\mathrm{d}r.

We may again divide the range of integration of the second term as
[0,\tau]=A\cup B
where
s+r,s^{\prime}+r
belong to the same maximal interval defining
\mathcal{S}_{\eta}
for
r\in A
, and to two different maximal intervals for
r\in B
. Then
taking expectations on both sides above and applying boundedness of
\bar{R}_{\theta},R_{\eta}
,
continuity of
R_{\eta}
to bound the integral over
r\in A
, and
|B|\leq C|s-s^{\prime}|
to bound the integral over
r\in B
, this shows

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathbb{E}\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s+\tau}}{\partial u^{s}}-\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}+\tau}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}\Big|\leq C\bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s+\tau}}{\partial u^{s}}-\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}+\tau}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}\Big|+\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathbb{E}\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s+r}}{\partial u^{s}}-\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}+\tau}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}\Big|\mathrm{d}r\bigg)+o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)

where
o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)
converges to 0 uniformly in
\tau
as
|s-s^{\prime}|\to 0
.
Then, since
\mathbb{E}|\frac{\partial\theta^{s}}{\partial u^{s}}-\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}|=0
, a Gronwall argument implies

\mathbb{E}\Big|\frac{\partial\theta^{s+\tau}}{\partial u^{s}}-\frac{\partial\theta^{s^{\prime}+\tau}}{\partial u^{s^{\prime}}}\Big|=o_{|s-s^{\prime}|}(1)
,
so also
s\mapsto R_{\theta}(s+\tau,s)
is continuous uniformly over
s\in[0,T]
and
\tau\in[0,T-s]
.
Thus
(s,t)\mapsto R_{\theta}(t,s)
is uniformly continuous over all

0\leq s\leq t\leq T
.

(
Condition for
\tilde{\alpha}
) By definition, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\tilde{\alpha}^{t}=\mathcal{G}(\tilde{\alpha}^{t},\mathsf{P}(\theta^{t}))

with
\tilde{\alpha}^{0}=\alpha^{0}
. The condition (
9
) and
boundedness of
C_{\theta}
shown above imply that

\alpha\mapsto\mathcal{G}(\alpha,\mathsf{P}(\theta^{t}))
is Lipschitz uniformly
over
t\in[0,T]
, so there exists a unique
solution
\{\tilde{\alpha}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
of this equation, which is uniformly continuous on

[0,T]
. Letting
C_{0}>0
be the constant defining
(
23
–
27
) which upper bounds
(
9
) of
Assumption
2.3
, and applying the above bound

\mathbb{E}(\theta^{t})^{2}=C_{\theta}(t,t)\leq\Phi_{C_{\theta}}(t)
, this solution satisfies
